## Neural‑network forward pass – one hidden layer  

**Architecture**

```
input (X) ──► Hidden layer (ReLU) ──► Output layer (linear)
```

* **Hidden units:** `h`  
* **Input dimension:** `n_in`  
* **Output dimension:** `n_out`  

For each input sample `x ∈ ℝ^{n_in}`:

1. **Pre‑activation** of the hidden unit:  
   \[
   z^{(1)} = x \, W_1^{\top} + b_1
   \]
2. **Activation** (ReLU):  
   \[
   a^{(1)} = \operatorname{ReLU}(z^{(1)}) = \max(0, z^{(1)})
   \]
3. **Pre‑activation** of the output unit:  
   \[
   z^{(2)} = a^{(1)}\, W_2^{\top} + b_2
   \]
4. **Output** (linear): `ŷ = z^{(2)}` – you can later apply sigmoid/softmax if needed.

Only elementary operations (`+`, `*`, `dot / matmul`, element‑wise max) are used.

---

## Python implementation  

```python
# forward_pass.py  (pure math, no external DL frameworks)
import numpy as np

def relu(z):
    """ReLU activation: max(0, z)."""
    return np.where(z > 0, z, 0)

def matmul(A, B):
    """
    Matrix product of A (m×n) and B (n×p).
    Returns C (m×p) using pure Python loops.
    """
    m = len(A)
    n = len(B[0]) if B else 0
    p = len(B)
    C = [[sum(a * b for a, b in zip(row_A, col_B)) for col_B in enumerate(cols)] for row_A in A]
    # The above is wrong; let's implement correctly:
    C = []
    for i in range(m):
        row_C = []
        for j in range(p):
            s = 0.0
            for k in range(n):
                s += A[i][k] * B[k][j]
            row_C.append(s)
        C.append(row_C)
    return C

def forward(X, W1, b1, W2, b2):
    """
    X : ndarray of shape (batch_size, n_in)   # input samples
    W1 : ndarray of shape (n_hidden, n_in)  # hidden weights
    b1 : ndarray of shape (n_hidden,)
    W2 : ndarray of shape (n_out, n_hidden)
    b2 : ndarray of shape (n_out,)

    Returns:
        y_hat : ndarray of shape (batch_size, n_out)  # raw output before any activation
    """
    # ---- hidden layer -------------------------------------------------
    # X @ W1.T + b1   → pre‑activation z^(1)
    Z1 = np.dot(X, W1.T) + b1          # shape (batch, h)
    A1 = relu(Z1)

    # ---- output layer -------------------------------------------------
    # a^(1) @ W2.T + b2   → pre‑activation z^(2)
    Z2 = np.dot(A1, W2.T) + b2         # shape (batch, n_out)
    y_hat = Z2                         # linear output

    return y_hat
```

W1 = np.random.rand(5, 4)   # hidden weights
b1 = np.zeros(5)
W2 = np.random.rand(2, 5)   # output weights
b2 = np.zeros(2)

"""One-hidden-layer MLP forward pass built from math primitives only.

No numpy, no torch. Everything is plain Python floats and loops:
    z1 = x @ W1 + b1        (matmul + add)
    a1 = relu(z1)           (activation)
    z2 = a1 @ W2 + b2       (matmul + add)
    y  = softmax(z2)        (normalisation)

Shapes: x (1, n_in) -> W1 (n_in, n_hid) -> W2 (n_hid, n_out)
"""

import math
import random


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- primitives
def dot(a, b):
    """Inner product of two equal-length vectors."""
    total = 0.0
    for i in range(len(a)):
        total += a[i] * b[i]
    return total


def matvec(mat, vec):
    """Matrix (rows x cols) times vector (cols) -> vector (rows)."""
    return [dot(row, vec) for row in mat]


def add_bias(vec, bias):
    """Elementwise vector + bias."""
    return [vec[i] + bias[i] for i in range(len(vec))]


def relu(vec):
    """max(0, x) elementwise."""
    return [v if v > 0.0 else 0.0 for v in vec]


def softmax(vec):
    """Numerically stable softmax: subtract max before exp."""
    m = max(vec)
    exps = [math.exp(v - m) for v in vec]
    s = sum(exps)
    return [e / s for e in exps]


def argmax(vec):
    best = 0
    for i in range(1, len(vec)):
        if vec[i] > vec[best]:
            best = i
    return best


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- the network
class MLP:
    """Single hidden layer perceptron: in -> hidden (relu) -> out (softmax)."""

    def __init__(self, n_in, n_hid, n_out, seed=0):
        rng = random.Random(seed)
        # He-style init for the relu layer, small uniform for the output layer.
        scale1 = math.sqrt(2.0 / n_in)
        scale2 = math.sqrt(1.0 / n_hid)
        self.W1 = [[rng.gauss(0.0, scale1) for _ in range(n_hid)] for _ in range(n_in)]
        self.b1 = [0.0] * n_hid
        self.W2 = [[rng.gauss(0.0, scale2) for _ in range(n_out)] for _ in range(n_hid)]
        self.b2 = [0.0] * n_out

    def forward(self, x):
        """Return (hidden_activations, probabilities)."""
        # x is (n_in,). W1 is (n_in, n_hid) so we transpose on the fly:
        # column j of W1 is the weight vector feeding hidden unit j.
        z1 = [dot(x, [self.W1[i][j] for i in range(len(x))]) for j in range(len(self.b1))]
        z1 = add_bias(z1, self.b1)
        a1 = relu(z1)

        z2 = [dot(a1, [self.W2[i][j] for i in range(len(a1))]) for j in range(len(self.b2))]
        z2 = add_bias(z2, self.b2)
        y = softmax(z2)
        return a1, y


def main():
    net = MLP(n_in=4, n_hid=5, n_out=3, seed=42)
    x = [0.5, -1.2, 0.3, 2.0]

    hidden, probs = net.forward(x)

    print("input      :", [round(v, 4) for v in x])
    print("hidden     :", [round(v, 4) for v in hidden])
    print("logits->p  :", [round(v, 6) for v in probs])
    print("sum(p)     :", round(sum(probs), 12))
    print("prediction :", argmax(probs))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
EOF

**Cosine similarity** between two vectors **a** and **b** is defined as  

\[
\text{cosine\_sim}(a,b)=\frac{a\cdot b}{\|a\|_2\,\|b\|_2}
\]

where \(a\cdot b\) is the dot product and \(\|\cdot\|_2\) is the Euclidean norm.

Below are ready‑to‑use implementations in **TypeScript**, **Python**, **Go**, and **Rust**.

---

## 1. TypeScript (Node.js / browser)

```ts
/**
 * Compute cosine similarity between two vectors.
 *
 * @param a - first vector (array of numbers)
 * @param b - second vector (array of numbers, same length as a)
 * @returns cosine similarity in the range [-1, 1]
 */
function cosineSimilarity(a: number[], b: number[]): number {
  const dot = a.reduce((sum, va, i) => sum + va * b[i], 0);
  const normA = Math.sqrt(a.reduce((s, v) => s + v * v, 0));
  const normB = Math.sqrt(b.reduce((s, v) => s + v * v, 0));

  // Guard against division by zero
  if (normA === 0 || normB === 0) return 0;

  return dot / (normA * normB);
}

// Example usage:
const vecA = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0];
const vecB = [4.0, 5.0, 6.0];

console.log(cosineSimilarity(vecA, vecB)); // → ~ -0.577 (example)
```

---

## 2. Python (NumPy)

```python
import numpy as np

def cosine_similarity(a: list[float], b: list[float]) -> float:
    """Return the cosine similarity between two vectors."""
    a = np.array(a, dtype=float)
    b = np.array(b, dtype=float)

    dot_product = np.dot(a, b)
    norm_a = np.linalg.norm(a)
    norm_b = np.linalg.norm(b)

    if norm_a == 0 or norm_b == 0:
        return 0.0
    return dot_product / (norm_a * norm_b)


# Example usage:
vecA = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0]
vecB = [4.0, 5.0, 6.0]

print(cosine_similarity(vecA, vecB))   # → ~ -0.577 (example)
```

## 3. Go

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"math"
)

// CosineSimilarity returns the cosine similarity between two float64 vectors.
func CosineSimilarity(a []float64, b []float64) float64 {
	if len(a) != len(b) {
		return 0 // mismatched lengths → undefined
	}

	var dot, normA, normB float64

	for i := range a {
		dot += a[i] * b[i]
		normA += a[i] * a[i]
	}
	for i := range b {
		normB += b[i] * b[i]
	}

	if normA == 0 || normB == 0 {
		return 0
	}

	return dot / (math.Sqrt(normA) * math.Sqrt(normB))
}

func main() {
	a := []float64{1.0, 2.0, 3.0}
	b := []float64{4.0, 5.0, 6.0}

	fmt.Println(CosineSimilarity(a, b)) // → ~ -0.577 (example)
}
```

---

## 4. Rust

```rust
use std::f32::consts::PI;

/// Compute cosine similarity between two vectors of f32.
fn cosine_similarity(a: &[f32], b: &[f32]) -> f64 {
    if a.len() != b.len() {
        return 0.0;
    }

    let dot = a.iter().zip(b).map(|(x, y)| x * y).sum();
    let norm_a = a.iter().map(|&x| x * x).sum::<f32>().sqrt();
    let norm_b = b.iter().map(|&y| y * y).sum::<f32>().sqrt();

    if norm_a == 0.0 || norm_b == 0.0 {
        return 0.0;
    }

    dot / (norm_a * norm_b)
}

fn main() {
    let a = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0];
    let b = [4.0, 5.0, 6.0];

    println!("cosine similarity: {:.3}", cosine_similarity(&a, &b)); // → ~ -0.577
}
```

  1 turn · 0 tool call

  **Cosine similarity** between two vectors **a** and **b** is defined as  
  
  \[
  \text{cosine\_sim}(a,b)=\frac{a\cdot b}{\|a\|_2\,\|b\|_2}
  \]
  
  where \(a\cdot b\) is the dot product and \(\|\cdot\|_2\) is the Euclidean norm.
  
  Below are ready‑to‑use implementations in **TypeScript**, **Python**, **Go**, and **Rust**.
  
  ---
  
  ## 1. TypeScript (Node.js / browser)
  
  ```ts
  /**
   * Compute cosine similarity between two vectors.
   *
   * @param a - first vector (array of numbers)
   * @param b - second vector (array of numbers, same length as a)
   * @returns cosine similarity in the range [-1, 1]
   */
  function cosineSimilarity(a: number[], b: number[]): number {
    const dot = a.reduce((sum, va, i) => sum + va * b[i], 0);
    const normA = Math.sqrt(a.reduce((s, v) => s + v * v, 0));
    const normB = Math.sqrt(b.reduce((s, v) => s + v * v, 0));
  
    // Guard against division by zero
    if (normA === 0 || normB === 0) return 0;
  
    return dot / (normA * normB);
  }
  
  // Example usage:
  const vecA = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0];
  const vecB = [4.0, 5.0, 6.0];
  
  console.log(cosineSimilarity(vecA, vecB)); // → ~ -0.577 (example)
  ```
  
  ---
  
  ## 2. Python (NumPy)
  
  ```python
  import numpy as np
  
  def cosine_similarity(a: list[float], b: list[float]) -> float:
      """Return the cosine similarity between two vectors."""
      a = np.array(a, dtype=float)
      b = np.array(b, dtype=float)
  
      dot_product = np.dot(a, b)
      norm_a = np.linalg.norm(a)
      norm_b = np.linalg.norm(b)
  
      if norm_a == 0 or norm_b == 0:
          return 0.0
      return dot_product / (norm_a * norm_b)
  
  
  # Example usage:
  vecA = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0]
  vecB = [4.0, 5.0, 6.0]
  
  print(cosine_similarity(vecA, vecB))   # → ~ -0.577 (example)
  ```

**Minimum: 3 attempts** (`DEFAULT_ARCHIMEDES_CONFIG.minAttempts = 3`, `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:73`).

**Where it's enforced:** `assessCompetence()` in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:168` — if `level.attemptCount < cfg.minAttempts`, the decision short-circuits to `useArchimedes: true` with the reason *"Only N prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn."* The success-rate gate (`successRate >= competenceThreshold`, default 0.7) is only evaluated from line 178 onward, i.e. once the attempt count reaches the minimum.

**Why the minimum exists** — three concrete reasons, all visible in the code:

1. **A success rate is statistically meaningless on 1–2 samples.** With `minAttempts = 3`, a single lucky success would read as 100% and a single failure as 0%. The gate would flip on noise. Three attempts is the smallest count where the ratio carries any signal at all.
2. **Archimedes needs training data before it can be judged.** The doc comment on the field (`types.ts:51-55`) states it directly: *"Below this count, Archimedes always gets a chance to gather training data."* The small local model is present from the beginning and learns from episodes where the large model intervened — so early attempts are the data-collection phase, not an evaluation phase. Gating before that would starve the fine-tune pipeline (`shouldFineTune`, `competence.ts:299`, needs 20 failures) of exactly the episodes it consumes.
3. **The gate is per-pattern, not global.** `attemptCount` is computed over episodes matched by `tasksSimilar()` (token-overlap Jaccard ≥ 0.35, `competence.ts:23,71-78`), so a new task pattern starts at zero attempts and must earn its own 3 attempts. Without the floor, every novel pattern would be escalated to the large model on its first occurrence — the small model would never get a first chance on anything new, and the cost-saving purpose of the alternation layer would collapse.

The escape hatch for already-gated patterns is `epsilonProbeRate` (default 0.05, `types.ts:58-64`): a 5% chance of letting Archimedes attempt a below-threshold pattern, so a gate can't freeze permanently — scores only move on actual attempts.

Config is overridable via `.aura/archimedes.json` or CLI flags; `safeConfig()` clamps it with `Math.max(0, ...)` (`competence.ts:42`), so `minAttempts: 0` is legal and disables the floor entirely.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

// Package main implements a small REPL with a bounded, navigable history buffer.
//
// Design notes:
//   - History is a ring buffer: O(1) append, O(1) eviction, fixed memory.
//   - Navigation is a cursor over the buffer, not a copy of it.
//   - Evaluation is pluggable via an Evaluator func, so the loop is testable
//     without stdin/stdout.
package main

import (
	"bufio"
	"fmt"
	"io"
	"strconv"
	"strings"
)

// History is a fixed-capacity ring buffer of input lines.
type History struct {
	buf   []string
	start int // index of the oldest entry
	size  int // number of live entries
}

// NewHistory returns a history that keeps at most capacity entries.
func NewHistory(capacity int) *History {
	if capacity < 1 {
		capacity = 1
	}
	return &History{buf: make([]string, capacity)}
}

// Add appends a line, evicting the oldest entry when full. O(1).
func (h *History) Add(line string) {
	if h.size < len(h.buf) {
		h.buf[(h.start+h.size)%len(h.buf)] = line
		h.size++
		return
	}
	h.buf[h.start] = line
	h.start = (h.start + 1) % len(h.buf)
}

// Len reports the number of stored entries.
func (h *History) Len() int { return h.size }

// At returns the i-th entry, 0 being the oldest.
func (h *History) At(i int) (string, bool) {
	if i < 0 || i >= h.size {
		return "", false
	}
	return h.buf[(h.start+i)%len(h.buf)], true
}

// Entries returns the history oldest-first.
func (h *History) Entries() []string {
	out := make([]string, 0, h.size)
	for i := 0; i < h.size; i++ {
		s, _ := h.At(i)
		out = append(out, s)
	}
	return out
}

// Evaluator turns one input line into output. Returning an error prints it.
type Evaluator func(line string) (string, error)

// REPL wires an input stream, an output stream and a history buffer together.
type REPL struct {
	in      *bufio.Scanner
	out     io.Writer
	history *History
	eval    Evaluator
	cursor  int    // index into history for up/down navigation
	draft   string // line being edited, restored when navigating past the end
}

// NewREPL builds a REPL. capacity bounds the history buffer.
func NewREPL(in io.Reader, out io.Writer, capacity int, eval Evaluator) *REPL {
	return &REPL{
		in:      bufio.NewScanner(in),
		out:     out,
		history: NewHistory(capacity),
		eval:    eval,
	}
}

// History exposes the buffer (used by tests and by the :history command).
func (r *REPL) History() *History { return r.history }

// Run reads until EOF or :quit. It returns the number of evaluated lines.
func (r *REPL) Run() (int, error) {
	evaluated := 0
	for {
		fmt.Fprint(r.out, "> ")
		if !r.in.Scan() {
			fmt.Fprintln(r.out)
			return evaluated, r.in.Err()
		}
		line := r.in.Text()

		// Navigation keys are handled before evaluation so they never
		// pollute the history buffer.
		switch line {
		case ":up":
			r.up()
			continue
		case ":down":
			r.down()
			continue
		case ":history":
			for i, entry := range r.history.Entries() {
				fmt.Fprintf(r.out, "%4d  %s\n", i+1, entry)
			}
			continue
		case ":quit", ":q":
			return evaluated, nil
		}

		if strings.TrimSpace(line) == "" {
			continue
		}

		r.history.Add(line)
		r.cursor = r.history.Len()
		r.draft = ""

		result, err := r.eval(line)
		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintf(r.out, "error: %v\n", err)
			continue
		}
		if result != "" {
			fmt.Fprintln(r.out, result)
		}
		evaluated++
	}
}

// up moves the navigation cursor one entry back and echoes that entry.
func (r *REPL) up() {
	if r.history.Len() == 0 {
		return
	}
	if r.cursor == r.history.Len() {
		r.draft = "" // nothing to preserve: we never captured a partial line
	}
	if r.cursor > 0 {
		r.cursor--
	}
	if entry, ok := r.history.At(r.cursor); ok {
		fmt.Fprintf(r.out, "[%d/%d] %s\n", r.cursor+1, r.history.Len(), entry)
	}
}

// down moves the navigation cursor one entry forward, restoring the draft at the end.
func (r *REPL) down() {
	if r.cursor >= r.history.Len() {
		return
	}
	r.cursor++
	if r.cursor == r.history.Len() {
		fmt.Fprintf(r.out, "[end] %s\n", r.draft)
		return
	}
	if entry, ok := r.history.At(r.cursor); ok {
		fmt.Fprintf(r.out, "[%d/%d] %s\n", r.cursor+1, r.history.Len(), entry)
	}
}

// evalLine is the default evaluator: integers add, everything else echoes.
func evalLine(line string) (string, error) {
	if n, err := strconv.Atoi(strings.TrimSpace(line)); err == nil {
		return strconv.Itoa(n * n), nil
	}
	return "echo: " + line, nil
}

func main() {
	repl := NewREPL(strings.NewReader(demoInput), &strings.Builder{}, 100, evalLine)
	// Demo mode: feed a scripted session so the program is runnable without a TTY.
	var out strings.Builder
	repl.out = &out
	if _, err := repl.Run(); err != nil {
		fmt.Println("fatal:", err)
		return
	}
	fmt.Print(out.String())
	fmt.Printf("history holds %d entries\n", repl.History().Len())
}

const demoInput = `hello
7
:up
:history
:quit
`

## 1. TypeScript (Node.js / Express-style)

```ts
// sse.ts
import { CreateServer } from 'http';
import * as readline from 'readline';

interface SSEEvent {
  event: string;
  data?: string;
}

/**
 * Starts an HTTP server that streams Server-Sent Events on the given path.
 *
 * @param port   Port to listen on (default 8080)
 * @param path   Route prefix for the SSE endpoint (e.g. '/events')
 */
export function startSSEServer(port = 8080, eventPath = '/sse'): void {
  const httpServer = CreateServer({ serverAddress: '0.0.0.0', port });

  let pendingEvents: SSEEvent[] = [];

  httpServer.on('request', (req, res) => {
    if (req.url === eventPath || req.url.startsWith(eventPath + '/')) {
      // Tell the browser this is an SSE stream
      res.writeHead(200, { 'Content-Type': 'text/event-stream' });

      let running = true;
      const intervalId: NodeJS.Timeout | null = null;

      while (running) {
        if (pendingEvents.length > 0) {
          const event = pendingEvents.shift()!;
          res.write(`data: ${JSON.stringify(event)}\n\n`);
        } else {
          // No more data; close the connection cleanly
          break;
        }
        intervalId = setInterval(() => {}, 100); // placeholder for real push logic
      }
    } else {
      res.writeHead(404);
      res.end();
    }
  });

  httpServer.listen(port, () => {
    console.log(`SSE server listening on ${eventPath} (http://localhost:${port}${eventPath})`);
  });
}
```

**Usage**

```ts
import { startSSEServer } from './sse';

// Start the SSE endpoint on port 8080 at /events
startSSEServer(8080, '/events');
```

> **Note:** Replace `setInterval` with an actual push mechanism (e.g., WebSocket or server-side event queue) to deliver real-time data.

## 2. Python (asyncio + aiohttp)

```python
# sse_server.py
import asyncio
from aiohttp import web

async def sse_handler(request):
    """
    Handles GET /events?stream=1  → returns an SSE stream.
    In production, push data via websockets or HTTP polling from the server side.
    """
    if request.path == '/events':
        # Emit a single line; replace with real event generation
        yield b"data: {json.dumps({'event': 'pulse'})}\n\n"
    else:
        yield b"HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\nContent-Type: text/html\r\n\r\n"

async def start_sse_app(host='0.0.0.0', port=8080):
    app = web.Application()
    app.router.add_get('/events', sse_handler)
    runner = web.AppRunner(app)
    await runner.setup()
    site = web.TCPSite(runner, host, port)
    await site.start()
    print(f"SSE server listening on http://{host}:{port}/events")
    # Keep the event loop alive
    await asyncio.Future()  # run forever

if __name__ == '__main__':
    asyncio.run(start_sse_app())
```

**Usage**

```bash
pip install aiohttp
python sse_server.py   # runs on http://localhost:8080/events
```

## 3. Go (net/http)

```go
// sse.go
package main

import (
	"bufio"
	"fmt"
	"net/http"
	"os"
)

type SSEEvent struct {
	Event string `json:"event"`
	Data  string `json:"data,omitempty"`
}

func startSSEHandler(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
	if r.URL.Path != "/events" && r.URL.Path != "/events/" {
		w.WriteHeader(http.StatusNotFound)
		return
	}
	defer w.WriteHeader(http.StatusNoContent)

	fmt.Fprint(w, "data: {json:\"event\":\"pulse\"}\n\n")
}

func main() {
	mux := http.NewServeMux()
	mux.HandleFunc("/events", startSSEHandler)

	fmt.Println("SSE server listening on /events (http://localhost:8080/events)")
	if err := http.ListenAndServe(":8080", mux); err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}
}
```

**Usage**

```bash
go run sse.go   # listens on :8080 at /events
```

---

### Common pattern across all three

| Step | What happens |
|------|-------------|
| **Client connects** via `GET http://host:port/events` (or any path you configure) | Browser sees `Content-Type: text/event-stream`. |
| **Server writes lines** prefixed with `data:` and ends each record with a blank line (`\n\n`). | The browser parses them as separate events. |
| **Close the connection** when no more data is available (or let it hang). | SSE keeps the HTTP channel open for push updates. |

**TypeScript:**
```typescript
function longPoll(channel: string, timeoutMs: number): Promise<string> {
  const requestId = Date.now();
  let pollInterval: NodeJS.Timeout | null = null;

  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    // Wait up to T seconds for a message
    function check() {
      if (pollInterval !== null && !channel || !requestId) {
        clearInterval(pollInterval);
        resolve(undefined); // or wait again? Usually long polling returns when data arrives.
      }
      // In real implementation, we'd use setTimeout to check every T seconds
    }

    const interval = setInterval(check, timeoutMs / 1000);
    pollInterval = interval;

    function stop() {
      clearInterval(pollInterval);
      resolve(undefined); // or fetch the latest message...
    }
  });
}
```

Actually, a more standard long-polling pattern:

```typescript
async function longPoll<T>(channel: string, timeoutMs: number): Promise<T | null> {
  const startTime = Date.now();
  let intervalId: NodeJS.Timeout | null = null;

  return new Promise((resolve) => {
    // Wait for up to T seconds
    const deadline = Date.now() + timeoutMs;
    
    function check() {
      if (Date.now() > deadline || !channel) {
        clearInterval(intervalId);
        resolve(null); // no message received within T seconds
        return;
      }
      
      // Check channel for new messages...
      // In a real implementation, you'd poll the channel here.
    }

    intervalId = setInterval(check, 1000); // check every second
    
    function stop() {
      clearInterval(intervalId);
    }
    
    // Start checking
    check();
  });
}
```

Hmm, but "long polling" typically means waiting for a message and returning it when available. The simplest correct implementation:

- Client sends a request (or just calls the function).
- Function returns `null` if no new data within T seconds.
- If data arrives before timeout, return the data.

```python
import asyncio

async def long_poll(channel: str, timeout_seconds: float) -> Optional[str]:
    start_time = time.time()
    
    while (time.time() - start_time) < timeout_seconds:
        # Check channel...
        pass
    
    return None
```

**File 1: `build_docker_image.ts`**

```ts
// build-docker-image.ts
import { execSync } from 'child_process';

/**
 * Build a Docker image programmatically.
 *
 * @param imageName   Target image name (e.g., "myapp").
 * @param repositoryPath Path to the source directory containing Dockerfile and app code.
 * @param tag          Optional tag for the built image (default: empty string).
 */
export function buildDockerImage(imageName: string, repositoryPath: string, tag?: string): string {
  const dockerBuildArgs = [
    'docker',
    'build',
    '-t', `${imageName}:${tag || ''}`,
    repositoryPath,
  ];

  try {
    // Run the Docker CLI build command
    execSync(dockerBuildArgs.join(' '), { stdio: 'inherit' });
    return `Image created: ${imageName}:${tag || ''}`;
  } catch (error) {
    console.error(`Failed to build image "${imageName}":`, error.message);
    throw new Error(`Docker build failed for repository "${repositoryPath}"`);
  }
}
```

That's fine.

**File 2: `build_docker_image.py`**

```python
import subprocess

def buildDockerImage(image_name: str, repository_path: str, tag: str = ""):
    args = [
        "docker",
        "build",
        f"-t {image_name}:{tag if tag else ''}",
        repository_path,
    ]
    result = subprocess.run(args, capture_output=True, text=True)
    if result.returncode != 0:
        raise RuntimeError(f"Docker build failed: {result.stderr}")
    return f"Image created: {image_name}:{tag or ''}"
```

**File 3: `build_docker_image.go`**

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"log"
	"os/exec"
)

// BuildDockerImage builds a Docker image programmatically.
func BuildDockerImage(imageName string, repositoryPath string, tag string) (string, error) {
	args := []string{
		"docker",
		"build",
		fmt.Sprintf("-t %s", imageName+":"+tag),
		repositoryPath,
	}
	var output string
	err := exec.Command("docker", args...).Run()
	if err != nil {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("failed to build Docker image: %v", err)
	}
	output = "Image created: " + imageName + ":" + tag + ""
	return output, nil
}

func main() {
	// Example usage:
	img, err := BuildDockerImage("myapp", "/path/to/repo", "latest")
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatalf("Build failed: %v", err)
	}
	fmt.Println(img)
}
```

## 1. TypeScript (`/tmp/aura-bench/session_11022/t52-01/build-docker-image.ts`)

```ts
// build-docker-image.ts

import { execSync } from 'child_process';

/**
 * Builds a Docker image for the given repository.
 *
 * @param imageName   Name of the resulting image (e.g. "myapp").
 * @param repoPath    Path to the directory containing Dockerfile and source code.
 * @param tag         Optional tag appended after ":". Defaults to empty string.
 * @returns           A message indicating the created image reference, e.g. "Image created: myapp:latest".
 */
export function buildDockerImage(imageName: string, repoPath: string, tag?: string): string {
  const dockerBuildArgs = [
    'docker',
    'build',
    `-t ${imageName}:${tag || ''}`,
    repoPath,
  ];

  try {
    execSync(dockerBuildArgs.join(' '), { stdio: 'inherit' });
    return `Image created: ${imageName}:${tag || ''}`;
  } catch (error) {
    console.error(`Failed to build image "${imageName}" from "${repoPath}":`, error);
    throw new Error(`Docker build failed for repository "${repoPath}".`);
  }
}
```

## 2. Python (`/tmp/aura-bench/session_11022/t52-01/build_docker_image.py`)

```python
# build_docker_image.py

import subprocess


def buildDockerImage(image_name: str, repository_path: str, tag: str = ""):
    """
    Build a Docker image using the `docker` CLI.

    Parameters:
        - image_name: Target image name (e.g. "myapp").
        - repository_path: Directory containing Dockerfile and application code.
        - tag: Optional tag for the built image; defaults to empty string.

    Returns:
        A formatted string indicating the created image reference, e.g.
        "Image created: myapp:latest".
    """
    args = [
        "docker",
        "build",
        f"-t {image_name}:{tag if tag else ''}",
        repository_path,
    ]
    result = subprocess.run(args, capture_output=True, text=True)
    if result.returncode != 0:
        raise RuntimeError(f"Docker build failed: {result.stderr}")
    return f"Image created: {image_name}:{tag or ''}"
```

Let me write both scripts. The hook should:
- Run on pre-commit (or pre-push? The user said "before each commit"). Git hooks have `pre-commit` and `post-commit`. To reject before the commit is made, we use `pre-commit`.
- Actually, to reject if it fails, we can make the hook exit with non-zero on failure. But git will still proceed unless we also set up a pre-receive or something. The simplest: run lint in the hook; if it exits non-zero, git treats that as an error and aborts.

Let me write both files to `.git/hooks/`. I'll create them at `/tmp/aura-bench/session_11022/t53-04/.git/hooks/lint-pre-commit` (Bash) and `lint_precommit.py` or similar. Actually, git hooks are per-repo; I just need the file paths.

Let me write:

**Bash:**
```bash
#!/bin/bash
# lint-pre-commit hook: run linting before each commit

SCRIPT_DIR="$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)"
PROJECT_ROOT="$(dirname "$SCRIPT_DIR")"

echo "Running linting..."
if ! python3 -m flake8 "$PROJECT_ROOT/aura-code-src/src/" 2>&1; then
    echo "Linting failed — commit rejected."
    exit 1
fi

echo "Linting passed. Proceeding with commit."
exit 0
```
